ParlaMint-NO_2014-12-09.ana.ud433e170	I think it's been a long time since the extended Committee on Foreign Affairs and Defence has had more meetings than we've had in the last year. The themes include: host: the humanitarian disaster and the deployment of Norwegian frigates to pick up chemical weapons from Syria ISIL’s advance and military contribution to Iraq, the stepping down of Norwegian forces’ contribution to Afghanistan and the continuation and strengthening of special forces’ training in Kabul Ukraine crisis and restrictive measures against Russia contributing to the disaster in the Central African Republic contribution of the Defence for the EU’s operation in Mali in the South Sudan bombing of Gaza Norwegian contribution to the fight against the ebola epidemic in West Africa summit in the NATO terrorist threat in summer and the general terror picture in the fall; [[CRU]] Many issues have been discussed, many decisions have been taken, and the Stortinget has been consulted and has been largely associated with the government on the main decisions. This dialogue is particularly important in a small country. I would also like to give my recognition to the three Councils for this good cooperation. But we could agree on much more. We should agree on one percent in aid to the world's poor and not set in motion a political theater every harvest. In the United Kingdom, conflicts between the right and the left are traditionally greater than in Norway, but through a deep economic crisis, they have agreed on a certain level of assistance, and they say that this consensus has led to a better debate on the quality of aid. We also need that in Norway – agree on a level of dignity and discuss how our assistance can be used more strategically. But, of course, the budget got better after the Christian People's Party and the Left got to say their own. That's 150 million. kr below what we think is the goal of achieving 1 pst., but it is not far from the aid target. We should also agree on good basic funding for more UN organisations and other organisations. Doctors Without Borders tells us that the Ebola crisis could be less extensive if the UN agencies had more funds at their disposal in the event of acute crises. When the outbreak came, the United Nations and other relief organizations had to start a beggar's way of travelling money. It took too much time for the international community to react. We should learn from that. The government should never cut the UN Food Fund for 2014, saying that they considered increasing the support when Refugee Aid and Norwegian People's Aid hit the alarm this summer, but waited for the crisis to come in December, before the money came on the table. These mistakes I see are trying to avoid until next year. And we should have agreed not to cut the U.D.'s cultural effort. There is little money that means a lot to Norway. I believe the focus on the education of girls in this budget is very important. Save the children and plan have done a lot to put this on the agenda in Norway, but I should like to see Norway be even more active in terms of reproductive health and women's issues in international forums. These are often controversial issues, and there are not so many who speak in international negotiations unless Norway does. The Labour Party agreed with the government's decision to send soldiers to Iraq. It was a difficult decision, but in this case I think it would be even harder to say no. ISIL is a threat to security throughout the region and the rest of the world. In many of the Middle East crises we see today, we don't have good answers, and extremists are getting more upset. But on the question of Israel and Palestine, we almost have a solution in the drawer. The answers are there. The two-state solution must be implemented, unless I am afraid that the danger of terrorism is increasing, and that moderate forces are losing out. I believe that we should follow those EU countries that advise against buying goods produced in occupied countries, because violations of international law are equally serious whether they are committed in Ukraine or they are committed in Palestine. The situation in Ukraine has led us to have a different relationship with Russia than a year ago. We support the restrictive measures as a protest against Russia's illegal annexation of Crimea. We also see military modernisation on the Russian side in the north. The NATO Neighbourhood Initiative underlines a stronger focus on Defence in our neighbourhoods. On the Norwegian side, we must ensure strong defence in the north and good surveillance of our seas. This is a priority in our alternative budget. But this is not an immediate threat to a bilateral military conflict with Russia in the north. To that end, we have too many common interests in maintaining the international legal order. Dialogue in the north is important now, and I am still surprised that the government in its Northern Region Strategy says they will go from dialogue to action. Dialogue is more important than ever. Our local politicians in the north play a very important role in safeguarding our foreign policy interests through dialogue and more cooperation projects with the Russian people. In today's defence policy priority, we must focus both on the threat of terrorism and on the ability of the defence to assist in any terrorist attack on Norway, while at the same time on our neighbourhoods, but the threat picture today underlines that the danger of possible terrorist attacks is most imminent. The debate and priorities should therefore reflect this fact. We need more knowledge about terrorism to meet this. The defence budget is close to following the ambition of the long-term plan adopted when the Workers' Party was in government, but not in the vicinity of the government party promised, which at the time believed that the long-term plan was underfunded. A growth of 1.43 billion. kr is a little less than 3.4 pst. There are more than many NATO countries have. The budget finances a large and announced increase in order to pay new fighter aircraft to the Defence. However, when the defence is subject to additional efficiency requirements both before and after the negotiations in Stortinget and the money goes right to the treasury and not to the priority business in Defense, it is no longer about efficiency improvement. It's a clean cut of over 100 million. kr. I record that the Minister of Defense every time she is asked about this, talks about the Labour Party in the eight years we were in government, rather than about her own budget and refers to her own budget as a historically high capacity pledge. I think that's easy to see through by people who are interested in defence policy. I don't think people in Defense recognize themselves in this description. No public agency has undergone similar changes as the Defence. My impression is that the people in the Defense loyally follow up the requirements for efficiency improvement imposed on them. What is perceived as the most unreasonable, however, are politicians who express increased ambitions, but who are not willing to pay for it. The budget now presented expresses higher ambitions in terms of operational capacity and military capacity, but instead of more money, more efficiency requirements are required. It increases the gap between rhetoric and reality. A new professional-military advice is announced, to be presented one week before the 2016 budget is due. It will be interesting to see whether this will be a priority for the government – whether it will be security and defence, as the Right and the Advance Party promised in the opposition, or whether there will be new tax cuts to be prioritised in the budget that will then be presented. I hope that in this area of defence policy we can work together on what we agree on: financing and following up the long-term defence plan. There is a great deal of agreement on the main lines of Norwegian foreign policy. But all the lines and choices should not be fixed in a time of change. It is an advantage of broad consensus, but consensus can also contribute to less discussion of our foreign policy priorities. I hope that today we have an open discussion on different aspects of Norwegian foreign policy priorities.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-12-09.ana.ud433e318	2014 has been a year of great tensions and changes in the security policy landscape. The year marking the 25th anniversary of the fall of the wall was to be the year of the return of security policy to Europe. We have seen that Russia, through the annexation of Crimea and support for the separatists in eastern Ukraine, has used military power against a neighbouring country to assert its own interests. We also see that humanitarian crises have worsened throughout the year. Long-term abuse of power and non-functioning state apparatus have been the source of popular discontent and after every cross-border crime and terrorism in several places in the world. Several of the conflicts in a number of countries in the Middle East and North Africa have now spread and become regional security crises with clear implications for Norwegian foreign and security policy. These changes have meant that Norway has had to make critical changes both in the aid facility and in the security policy. I am therefore pleased that the government has presented a development budget and a defence budget that reflects the changes in security policy and development policy, with clear changes in direction and priorities. I am pleased that there is broad consensus in the broad lines of foreign and defence policy. In the opinion of the Right, the work of the Committee on the Budget for the Year has been clear and consensual, in line with the Norwegian foreign policy tradition. International law is particularly important for Norway as a small country with significant sea areas and strong interests in the north. It is therefore important that the UN Charter applies to all countries. To the right, it is important to clarify that Ukraine has been an independent country since 1991 in line with the UN Charter. The reintroduction of spheres of influence by Russia and the imposition of claims on the territory of neighbouring countries are unacceptable developments. I am therefore pleased that a joint committee stresses that developments in Russia have gone in a disturbing direction in recent years, where the development of democracy, human rights and civil society are under increasing pressure. The Committee also agrees that Russia’s illegal annexation of Crimea has triggered a serious security policy situation in Europe, which also has implications for Norway’s relations with Russia, including the restrictive measures adopted by the EU, the United States and other countries. That the government is now proposing to strengthen support for, among other things. Rule of law reform, good governance and human rights in countries such as Ukraine, Moldova and Georgia in this budget, are a necessary and fundamental step and fit into the perspective of the long-term democratisation of the countries of the region. The Nordic regions are our most important area of foreign policy interest. It is therefore gratifying that the government’s North Sea bet increases by over half a billion dollars to nearly three billion dollars. kr for 2015. It is now important that we are able to exploit the knowledge we have built up in the north, so that new businesses and jobs are created. It is also positive that the government in the budget states that Barents' cooperation will continue. People-to-people cooperation with Russia and cooperation in areas such as fisheries management, the environment, nuclear safety, research and search and rescue are important to maintain. Continued cooperation with all neighbouring countries in the Arctic is important. Everyone has served a peaceful coexistence in the north, and the Committee therefore agrees that NATO must seek a good cooperation relationship with Russia. I have also had the pleasure of being the case rapporteur for the Svalbard budget and I can see broad consensus and support for the government's proposal. It is important that Norway's presence in the northern regions is given a higher priority, including in the defence budget. The 2015 Defence Budget is SEK 43.8 billion. kr – a relatively high level. With a real growth of almost 1.5 billion. kr – about 3.3 pst. – in the opinion of the Right, this is a realistic budget which, at the same time, is characterised by clear priorities in the needs that are coming forward. The Government follows up and finances the objectives and main direction of the current long-term plan in the draft budget for 2015. The government strengthens operational activity and preparedness in all defence sectors and in the Home Office. At the same time, it has been possible to track expenditure related to the acquisition of combat aircraft and the development of the Joint Strike Missile without jeopardizing other parts of the military's operational capability. I am pleased that there is a majority in the Committee on these main priorities. The budget also provides an important boost to overall preparedness and social security through enhanced helicopter preparedness in Back and Bardufoss. After a real decline in four of the eight defence budgets of the previous government, it is interesting that the Labour Party believes that the budget is inadequate. To that end, we need to go back to 2003 to find a larger real increase in the budget. We now have one problem to do together. It is a fact that the defence budgets have been maintained at some level in recent years. In dialogue with the defence sector, government and enlargement must facilitate the achievement of the objectives both in terms of migration and in terms of the next long-term. Parts of the long-term plan will need to be adapted in line with changes in the security policy threat picture and changes in the planning assumptions. The professional military investigation the head of defence has now been commissioned to carry out is a good start to such a process. On the right, support the job done by the government to prioritize and modernise our military capabilities so that the defence builds good operational capability at home and can take its share of responsibility for handling crises internationally. In recent years, many allies have had to make significant reductions. As you can see, Norwegian defence is in a better position than many other countries. It is important that we work closely with our allies in the further process. Close to the southern border of Europe there is a very dramatic development. The extremist terrorist organisation ISIL must be combated. The brutal advance of ISIL in Iraq and Syria is causing great suffering, and they have taken territorial control of large parts of the countries. It is absolutely necessary to put in place a political solution in both Iraq and Syria. In Syria, we see one of the greatest humanitarian crises since World War II. It is estimated that 197,000 people have been killed since the start of the conflict in 2011, of which 62 000 are civilians. 3 million people are displaced, and half of the country's population depends on humanitarian aid. The neighbouring countries are under great pressure and accept huge refugee flows. Lebanon, a country with 4 million inhabitants, has received over 1.1 million refugees. Norway is one of the largest contributors with a total of 845 million. kr in response to the crisis in Syria in 2014, in addition to SEK 200 million. kr to Iraq. A significant increase of 424 million. kr for humanitarian efforts and a high level of the aid budget ensure sound Norwegian efforts in 2015 as well. Norway is at the top of the world in terms of assistance. The budget for 2015 is going to be the largest ever, about 33 billion. kr including climate and forest bets. We see a historical commitment to education, health, international climate cooperation, humanitarian aid, business and efforts to eradicate all extreme poverty. The aid budget is also historic because aid is being reformed. The OECD has criticised Norway for lubricating the aid for thin outside. The most important thing is what we get out of the money, that Norwegian aid works, and that we achieve concrete results. Therefore, efforts are concentrated from 116 countries to 84 countries. In the Committee on Foreign Affairs and Defence there is broad agreement on a prudent concentration of assistance. Humanitarian aid and non-governmental organisations are not affected and derogations may be granted. Concentration shall not prevent Norwegian humanitarian action in crisis situations and such flexibility is necessary. For example, the Ebola epidemic has created an exceptional crisis situation in Sierra Leone and Guinea, which did not receive Norwegian assistance in 2013. Norway will contribute to the reconstruction, which will require considerable efforts. In order to ensure development in the poorest countries, we need to focus on development cooperation, trade policy, good investment and job creation conditions. Aid alone is not enough. Sustainable economic growth is crucial for the path out of poverty. Therefore, the government wants to invest more in business development with an increase of 290 million. kr. 250 million. kr of this increase goes to the State-owned investment fund Norfund, which primarily invests in high-risk renewable energy and agriculture projects in developing countries. Nor should Norwegian aid come instead of their own efforts or compensate for bad policies. Both we as donors and the people of developing countries expect to see developments that promote good governance and respect for human rights. By combating corruption and establishing a good investment climate, we contribute to a development that ensures tax revenue for common welfare. With the budget for next year, we're taking a power cap for education. Education for all is not just a human right; it is a condition for growth and development. 58 million children today are out of school. Children affected by war and conflict, girls and people with disabilities are particularly vulnerable, and here we are raising our efforts considerably. After just one year in government, we have increased the investment in education by 640 million. kr. These are significant changes and a shift in the pace of Norwegian foreign policy. Norway’s foreign and development policy shall have clear priorities, be anchored in liberal values and have as its primary objective the safeguarding of central national interests. The long lines of Norwegian security and defence policy are clear: the essential importance of the UN and international law NATO as the foundation of our cooperation with neighbours, friends and partners in the North and Europe our presence in the North In addition, it is important that Norway assumes responsibility for ensuring results in development policy. With the 2015 budget, I believe we can safely say that we are pursuing these objectives through strengthened efforts in key areas such as the North, Education, Health, Humanitarian Aid and Industrial Development.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-12-09.ana.ud433e655	In the last year, we've seen a series of humanitarian crises happening at the same time. About one in three people in sub-Saharan Africa are today defined as undernourished. In this area, food production has been reduced in recent years, and in many countries there is little room for expanding agricultural land. The Food and Agriculture Organisation of the United Nations, FAO, indicates that Africa has imported about a quarter of its food needs over the last few years. With increasing population density, existing food production is being divided into more and more. At the same time, the earth will be depleted. There's a hurry to start a development where productivity increases, based on a resilient management of the earth. There is great uncertainty about future prices on the international food market. If both accessing and paying for food becomes more difficult, national self-sufficiency will be more important for each country. If food supply on the international food market is reduced, it is all the more important to strengthen agriculture and the ability of each country to produce its own food. Among other things, the World Bank has indicated that the most effective way to reduce poverty is to support small farmers in developing countries. There are no other industries that can compare with agriculture when it comes to creating employment and economic distortions for the very poorest in the world. Norway has a high level of competence in food production. Strengthening agriculture's share of the aid budget could bring important health benefits as well as economic and social benefits to the beneficiary countries. The government proposed to increase the aid from Norway by around 870 million. kr. This was about 420 million. kr less growth than the government’s proposals for growth in transfers in EEA funds. The centre party will help people get out of trouble, silt and poverty. That's the sensible use of tax money for people. I think the EEA funds have little to do with this. Among other things, Europe’s Communication showed that much of Norway’s payments in EEA funds are in support of cultural heritage and environmental projects, and a large number of questions about whether – and, if so, to what extent – the objective of living conditions is to smooth out. In its 2013 report, the National Audit Office writes that it is uncertain to what extent it can be demonstrated that the Norwegian EEA funds contribute to economic and social cohesion. The same question is asked by the Center Party. Is it effective reduction of poverty to allow funds to a Slovak mine museum, a Polish maritime museum in Gdansk, the refurbishment of a church bell in Budapest or the renewal of the square in Krakow? The central party believes that Norway's payments to EEA funds are not related to the development of the permissions of the poorest among us. For the period 2009–2014, Poland received more than 50 times as much in EEA funds as e.g. Sierra Leone received Norwegian aid between 2009 and 2013. In Sierra Leone, large parts of the population live in absolute poverty. The same is not the case in Poland. Poland received more EEA funds for 2009–2014 than the 80 beneficiary country received together in Norwegian assistance from 2009 to 2013. Last week I asked for an overview of all the projects that Norway contributed to financing through the EEA funds. I received a long list of more than 650 pages, so I had enough reading material this weekend. As the government is negotiating a new round of payments of EEA funds, it is entirely necessary for the Norwegian people to see the amount of Norway’s rights in EEA funds and the use of these funds. The feedback I have received recently indicates that there are a lot of Norwegian people who are not informed about the size of the payments or what Norway is authorising the EEA funds to do. Norway must always be able to defend our freedom as an independent nation. It's a completely fundamental task for every nation, and it's our responsibility as elected representatives to do this. There is reason to point out that the international situation has developments that give cause for concern. In 2014, we have experienced extensive, sharp military operations in the heart of Europe for the first time since 1945. In the last year, we have seen several conflicts occur in different places at the same time. This can happen again. NATO may, in the future, experience a situation in which several Member States are faced with different approaches at the same time. Then it is absolutely necessary that the defense has a volume and a capacity to be persistent enough to defend Norway over a longer period of time. At the same time, a solid defense would be horrifying. Sufficient investment in Defence reduces the risk of attacking Norway. The main task of defence is to defend Norwegian territory. This is a task that requires great resources and political priority. Norway has a land area of size with Italy, but with one tenth of the population, and we're going to defend a sea area that has the same size as the Mediterranean. We have a coast rich in energy resources and fish. Resource-rich areas are traditionally more exposed to conflicts, which requires a conscious strategy for Norwegian defence policy. The government has announced that they will step up Norway's military presence in the north. The center party thinks it's important to have a solid presence in the north. However, there is reason to ask if the government's rhetoric is in line with the government's rights to the Defence. In order to strengthen the Defence in the North, it is absolutely necessary to strengthen the Maritime Defense and Coast Guard. Today we know that the Maritime Defense experience great challenges related to having enough crew and taxes on the crew. Norway’s Officers’ Association points out that maritime defence, and in particular the Marine, have major challenges related to staff and operations. During the hearing of the defence budget, it was also pointed out that if today's agreed task portfolio and structure are to be maintained, the licenses of the Maritime Defense Service must be increased. In the opposition, the Right and Advance Party pointed out that the maritime defence had a difficult resource situation, which, in the view of the parties, had to be addressed immediately and which would require extensive action in the short and long term. It is difficult to see that the Right Party and the Advance Party have followed this in government. The Coast Guard is also facing a challenging resource situation. The Defence Budget will result in a reduction in the activity level of the Coast Guard under the agreed level of ambition and in a reduction in the efficiency of the vessel structure. The centre party means the signal from different actors in the defense gives cause for some concern. The Defence Manager is concerned about operational capability, which we can read in the proposition is reduced for parts of the Defence. We therefore suggest increasing the rights of the defence. In the Central Party's alternative budget, we strengthen Coast Guards by 17.5 million. kr. We're increasing the licenses for Marine Defense by $25 million. kr. We'll allow 50 million. kr more for the Army and 30 million. kr more to Homeland. In order for young people to learn how to use weapons safely and to ensure that the Home Security is a good path for shooting around the country, the Voluntary Sniper Service is making a great effort. The centre party has therefore proposed in our alternative budget to increase their licenses by 2.5 million. kr. We also expect the government to provide the organisation with good and good conditions for further development, not least in relation to previous announcements about this great organisation. For the Central Party, it is important that, through a concerted effort, we prioritise enough resources to ensure a solid defence of our country for a troubled period of time. The Central Party, through its alternative budget, shows the clearest willingness to prioritize the Norwegian defence and to maintain such a strong stance as a solid defence party.

Today, as the representative Skei Grande has already been so lovable to show, I would like to mention one example, namely the development of the future of Svalbard. While the Norwegian state company is developing coal on Svalbard, the government spends a lot of time and effort telling the EU and the rest of the world that they will stop using coal and buy Norwegian gas instead. At the same time, the relationship with Russia is still used as an argument for Norwegian coal operations on Svalbard. It is time to do something about such a fossil foreign policy thinking, which helps to maintain a fossil and climate hostile activity. The limit was really exceeded when the government-appointed Skancke Committee last week used Norwegian coal activities on Svalbard as an argument against drawing State pension funds abroad from coal. We have to get out of this backyard. The time is ripe to end the Norwegian coal industry on Svalbard, which is now also losing, and to make the Svalbard community the world's most sustainable polar society. That is why we are tabling two proposals today: We will ask the government to start planning to further develop the Svalbard community with emphasis on sustainability, environmental research, a renewable energy base and new economic pathways. And we will ask the government to ensure that the coal mining operations on Svalbard are closed when the current mines are driven out. Svalbard has many others, good legs to stand on. The archipelago has a bright future without coal. This matter is a test of our will and ability to rethink both the climate and business in the north and the neighbourhood of Russia in the Arctic, where a forward-looking policy takes over coal mines as a symbol of Norwegian sovereignty. I would like to bring this up with the Greens' two proposals.

The world looks different today than it did a year ago, which has been attended by several representatives. I would like to take this opportunity to thank the members of the Committee, and not least the chairman of the Committee, for what I have experienced as a very good cooperation in a difficult time. At a time when fundamental principles of our world order are being challenged, it is vital that we hold fast to what has brought us prosperity, prosperity, and security. That is why we stick to our friends and allies and continue to engage new actors. We hold our freedom and security high, and our stand is the UN, NATO and Europe. We stand for democracy, human rights and binding rules of law. We believe in cooperation and free trade. Russia’s violation of international law and the destabilisation of Ukraine spread uncertainty in Europe. The cohesion of NATO and the European Great Family has made it possible to provide Russia with a clear response that violations of international law will not pay off. Through economic and other cooperation, we will continue to support Ukraine's democratic course. The brutality of ISIL and other extremists shocks us. It is destabilising countries and regions on a terrifying scale, causing unimaginable suffering. We're going to help fight ISIL militarily, politically, ideologically and economically. Norway shall continue to be among the largest humanitarian contributors to Syria. It is essential that we assist neighbouring countries that accept large refugee flows from Syria. It is particularly important to strengthen the capacity of education to prevent us from losing a generation of Syrian children who fall outside the school system. Norway contributes significantly here, but it will be important to further increase efforts. No shock generation of Syrian children! The humanitarian needs of the world have increased dramatically. Today we are facing four humanitarian crises, defined as level 3 of the UN. Several representatives have been involved in this in a good and solid way. And there are great and unmet needs. It has to be reflected in the budget so that we can respond quickly and efficiently. By increasing humanitarian aid, as we have proposed both in the revised national budget, in the new age and in the next year's budget, we are better able to contribute to both acute and long-term crises. Next year, we're proposing an increase of 424 million. kr, more than SEK 3,3 billion. Total kr. The Ebola epidemic has shown that we also need to mobilise to prevent pandemics. It has also reminded us of the link between health and safety and between long-term development and emergency aid. I am pleased that there is broad support for a strong commitment to education. Education is a human right and a condition for growth, development and jobs. After just one year, the government has strengthened the education sector by 640 million. kr, from SEK 1,75 billion. kr to close to 2.4 br. kr. We will also further develop and increase Norway's efforts and global leadership role for health by nearly 3 billion. kr in support next year. In addition, it is worth reflecting that last year, at the same time, we had very limited funds dedicated to global climate funding. In the government proposal, we've added up to 200 million. kr in funding until next year. In Stortinget, in cooperation with the Left and Christian People's Party, it's put at 200 million. kr. Norway will grant 400 million. kr next year, but I've also made it clear that the next four years will be at least at this level, 400 million. NOK, so we commit to SEK 1.6 billion. kr in the coming period. If we look at the promises given both now in Lima and the promises made at the donor country meeting, Norway is at the top, if not at the top, with this promise – in relation to the population. I think it's worth including in a debate where it's presented as if we might not do that much. We're doing a lot, but the needs are great, so we'll of course keep assessing, as the representative Skei Grande was about, how we're going to contribute more if we can get more. In addition to humanitarian aid, we give priority to education, health and vaccines, industrial development, equality, climate and human rights. Development cooperation must deliver concrete results for the poorest. We focus efforts on a smaller number of countries, while strengthening efforts in twelve focus countries. At the same time, we stress that this concentration should not include non-governmental organisations and humanitarian aid. We will come back to Stortinget to anchor this important process further. In the northern regions, Norway is a major act, and we have important interests to care for. Extensive north-eastern diplomacy and international cooperation in the Arctic are more important than ever. Among other things, we see: to cooperate with the United States, which is the current presidency of the Arctic Council. We've proposed Northern Region measures for almost three billion. kr in 2015. That's an increase of well over half a billion dollars, $574 million. kr, based on the calculation method used by the previous government. I'm the first to admit that this calculation method has its sides, but it at least illustrates that it's a willingness to continue the bet in the north and also increase it. We see more clearly than ever that foreign policy becomes domestic policy. This is illustrated by epidemics, refugee flows, terrorist threats and foreign fighters, but also through an open economy and cross-border cooperation. It is a strength for Norway that there is a cross-political consensus on the broad lines of foreign policy, which I was in the midst of. The 2015 budget takes care of our long-term interests and makes us more resilient in times of crisis. With this budget, we can continue to contribute internationally with our allies. This budget allows Norway to take international leadership in key areas such as health, education and humanitarian matters. The challenges facing the world in 2015 are great, but I believe that we will adopt a budget in the Ministry of Foreign Affairs' responsibilities that is well adapted to the geopolitical challenges we see and the humanitarian challenges that the world is experiencing.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-12-09.ana.ud433e1202	No international organisation is more important for Norway than the EU. No cooperation means more for Norwegian everyday life than the EU, and no market is more important for Norwegian enterprises than the European market. This is why Norwegian foreign policy begins in Europe, and that is why there is also a broad consensus on Norwegian European policy in this Chamber. I would like to thank the Committee and its chairman for the good cooperation that has taken place this year. The EEA provides predictability for Norwegian companies in what is our home market. 80 pst. Of the goods we sell, go to the EU, 65 pst. of the goods we buy come from the EU. 60 pst. Norwegian investment goes to the EU, 70 pst. of foreign investment in Norway comes from the EU and 7 pst. of all employed persons in Norway are workers from the EU. It goes without saying that it is important for Norway how this domestic market develops. Europe is now facing an economic situation that calls for more growth and more competitiveness. This will require major structural reforms in many countries, greater labour market mobility will be required, responsible and long-term economic policies will be required, and necessary incentives both at national and European level will be required. In the global picture, the negotiations on TTIP, the Transatlantic Trade and Investment Agreement, could be of great importance. Free trade over the Atlantic can be a vitamin injection both for the transatlantic region and for the world economy. The alternative to new growth is deeper economic and social problems in Europe and a Europe that is becoming weaker in the world. It's not in Norway's interest. Norway contributes to the fight against economic and social inequalities in the least prosperous EU country. The EEA funds are a Norwegian solidarity contribution. There is no quota for participation in the EEA, it is a solidarity contribution where we contribute to reducing social and economic disparities in the most vulnerable areas of the EU. But these funds also help to strengthen the internal market, because by creating more growth, by creating more equal conditions in Europe, we also strengthen the functioning of the internal market. There is one background that the government in the next programming period will want to place greater emphasis on measures for innovation, growth and jobs in the device of EEA funds than we have seen in the past. As I said, this is not quota money. They are not going to the European Union either, but to individual countries. But the EEA funds are the result of an international agreement, they are the result of an agreement that gives substance to the term of honor we often use, i.e. binding international cooperation. I'm recording that the Center Party wants to break this agreement for next year by cutting $2.50. kr in it. Breaking a binding international agreement with an international organisation of such importance to Norway is unwise. Wanting to do so in the same year as we are in difficult negotiations with the EU on the next agreement period for EEA funds, is – yes, President, it almost fails to characterise itself. We are negotiating new EEA funds. These are difficult negotiations that have been going on since January of this year. As you know, the EU requires a sharp increase in our contribution, which we consider unreasonable, as their own resources for social economic development only have a minor increase in the current budgetary period. There should be no doubt about Norway’s willingness to continue and its willingness to contribute. We are pleased that the negotiations are going on again, but the distance is still very great. We hope to get ahead of them now, precisely because these contributions can play a role in balancing living conditions in Europe, and precisely because we want – in the next period – to focus more on growth-enhancing measures in the recipient countries.

This is a result of conscious political priorities, and it is also an expression of the functioning of policy and international cooperation. ParlaMint-NO_2014-12-09.ana.ud433e1312	There is a great deal of agreement in Norway about our aid budget, because it has to be large. But after the agreement with the Christian People's Party and the Left, the increase is another nearly 500 million. So it's going to be $33 billion. kr. Until last year, Norway’s assistance was smearingly thin - up to 116 out of 143 possible countries. The OECD review of Norwegian aid addressed the issue of a recommendation to strengthen performance orientation and concentrate efforts. Many of our large aid organisations have already done so. Careful concentration of assistance and also focus on 12 focus countries is a significant change in Norwegian aid policy. This is done to achieve better and more measurable results and more effective assistance. The most important thing is what comes out of the use of money and the effort – results that show that Norwegian aid works. The government will come back to Stortinget with a case about this. It is worth noting that this geographical concentration does not include the work done by non-governmental organisations. Nor shall the concentration prevent Norwegian humanitarian action in crisis situations or exclude the possibility of supplementing emergency assistance with longer-term prevention assistance to prevent crises. Education is essential for eradicating extreme poverty. Millions of children are still out of school today. Children affected by war and conflict, girls and disabled people are particularly vulnerable. Government parties will pay particular attention to the education of girls. Girls who receive education, get married later, have children later, have more opportunity to get a job and get better health for themselves and for their own children. In sub-Saharan Africa, infant mortality is estimated to have been reduced by up to 40 pst. if the mothers had had had proper schooling. Education is the key to informed choices later in life and affects relationships such as child marriage, sexual mutilation, child mortality and general health in the population. Focusing on education in crisis and conflict situations is not least an investment in stability, democracy and development. With additional funds allocated to this, the government’s objective is to contribute to the access of one million more children living in crisis and conflict situations to quality education up to 2017. And the very important thing: people with disabilities are the most marginalized in education. This group shall be prioritised. When we then know that there are hundreds of thousands of jobs in different countries in Europe that depend on trade with Russia, it is in everyone's interest that we should seek a diplomatic solution to this conflict the sooner, the better, which means that someone has to talk – in other words, more dialogue and more action. I have mentioned that economic policy is a security policy, we see that, but I must also agree with the Minister for Foreign Affairs when he highlights the importance of global health promotion measures being also linked to security policy aspects. The same applies to the climate challenges, and they will hit us all. We've seen the latest pictures from the Philippines, and we have a strong memory of what happened in the West a short time ago, and not least we also see that Ebola is something we have to deal with here in the country. That is why I believe that both health and climate policies must be raised more clearly when defence and security policies are discussed in the national and international arena.

So far, we are following the current long-term plan. The new professional-military council will be able to change the professional recommendations and thus have implications for budgetary priorities in the coming years. As a result of the change in the security picture, we need a strong defence with a satisfactory operational capability, so that we can defend the country and stand by the alliance obligations. We have to take the consequences of this, and prepare for the need to give Defence greater priority in the years to come.

In spring 2016, the Norwegian local government will take important decisions, and it is important to stress that it is the local government itself that determines the type of decision they want to take, but after a good and thorough process and – which is an important point – one year after Stortinget has indicated what tasks the local government will be doing in the future. The debate on this state budget has been very much about the social profile. For the Progress Party, social commitment is important. We're busy helping those who need the most public stuff. We are concerned that our children should have an upbringing in kindergartens with a high level of adding them and in a school where their children are motivated to develop Norway further. Then we are occupied with good care for the elderly and nursing, and it is not only about care and care, but also about the provision of cultural activities, good food and activities, so that the elderly get their star hours. Good health care is also important when disease strikes or when children need guidance at a health centre. These are many of the functions for which Norwegian municipalities are responsible, and which are important for our inhabitants, and therefore it is important for us to strengthen the social profile, to strengthen the community economy. Therefore, the Progress Party used its power in Stortinget in the period 2001-2005 to strengthen the municipal economy, and that is why we also use our ability as a government party to strengthen the municipal economy – not because it should be possible to build more cultural houses or town halls, but because the municipalities should be equipped to provide good supplies to those who need it most. For this reason, it is gratifying to register the Budget Survey to KS, which points out that the areas that are most strongly strengthened underpin the social profile, namely that child protection and the care sector are some of the areas that receive the most growth. Elles reports to the municipalities that there is demanding budgetary work – as it was last year, and as it has been for about every year. Many of the explanations are that municipalities will do a lot. They want to invest a lot, and they do their priorities. This has always been the case in the municipal sector, and so is this year. The biggest difference between now and before is that the Storting recognizes that it is. We are not here today to express that the municipalities have never received more money: even if they have never received more money, we say that being a municipality requires priorities, and being a municipality requires a priority between different offers that you want to strengthen. I would particularly like to thank the Christian People's Party and the Left for the cooperation we have had on this budget – both in the Financial Committee and in the Municipal Committee. They will certainly do well for their hearts and their permeability later in the debate. The Progress Party has also had a strong impact and has seen its footprint in this budget. One of the most important points for us is that we have strengthened the growth community by lowering the point of entry to 1.6 pst, so that 68 municipalities get growth municipal support for next year, compared to 24 this year. These are municipalities that have major challenges with an increasing population and therefore require large investments to provide them with a supply. To spring, the government in the municipal bill will come up with an assessment of different models for returning parts of the corporate tax to the municipalities. This is important in order to increase the tax morals of businesses, which will be the case when they see that the value creation becomes local again, rather than going to Oslo for further distribution. The budget has also been strengthened following the legalisation of the BPA offer. We stress that it is important that municipalities provide real choice for users when implementing the BPA scheme. Differentiated employer fees have our theme in this state budget of the Committee. This is complicated, but it is easy to say that the principle is such that this system should primarily apply to business, not to municipal activities. It is therefore logical and correct that municipalities should not benefit from a lower fee and that that benefit should be taken into account in the framework. The same principle has been applied since 2004 – by changing governments. It is also true that some people have been compensated because the fees were set up in 2004 and 2007. I think everyone agrees that no one should have compensation for a disadvantage that they no longer have, but we see that the statesmen who follow that compensation scheme have had a slightly different practice. The majority in the committee have asked the department to ensure that the flexibility chosen by many states to reduce this compensation over time is also exercised for more. Finally, I have to enter the county councils: they have had a great deal of focus in the processing of this budget because one has established new cost keys on the income system and has corrected the wrong conditions that have been seen in the old system. Because the red-green government left the income system in a drawer, the changes have been big because one has waited to do them. That's why we also agree that it's important to have a loss compensation that will last for several years, so that the changes for those who lose will be easier than if you had made them right away. So we're happy that it's set at 185 million. kr, but we remind you that it says in the settings from the majority that county councils are not allowed to use this loss compensation to defer the necessary adjustments to their expenses to new income frameworks. There is one element of the new income system that will be considered in the future in conjunction with the municipality's 2016 proposal, namely boat and ferry criteria. Here the departments shall evaluate the data material more closely, and one shall consider how to introduce a holiday relief scheme, as requested by the Committee in the context of the municipal proposal. It is clear that this review could lead to changes and redistributions between municipalities. This is a work that is important to follow, and it is important that the county councils also help to obtain the data needed to make good analysis and good estimates of the differences in costs between different county municipalities relating to boat and ferry, which have proved to be one of the most difficult elements to set a good criterion in the new income system. That is why one will continue to work on this. In these comments, the Committee's plurality indicates that new offers for ferries are becoming more expensive. Therefore, we underline the importance of a more frequent update of cost keys and income systems than usual, in order to capture new expenditures more quickly. The budgetary agreement between the government parties and the Christian People's Party and the Left further strengthens the local economy. The next year's growth in free income is about 4.7 billion. kr. This enables Norwegian municipalities to strengthen important welfare services and close gaps in the social security network. Growth is about the same level as in the last decade. In fact, if we look at the growth of free incomes after taking into account population developments, demographics, growth in 2015 is greater than it was in 2011, greater than in 2012 and greater than in 2013 — under the red-green government — and far greater than that adopted in 2014. The real economic space for the municipal sector is growing in 2015 than it has been in recent years. Next year they'll increase even more. NOK to nearly 400 million. kr next year. It also means strengthening the municipalities as a foundation for the rule of law. There is no light on, neither landowners nor nature values. Environmental and land management must build on local self-government and respect for property law to a far greater extent. The relationship between use and protection of nature is important. In most cases, active business is the same as defence-related natural management, and the concerns of individuals and communities need to be addressed better. We have a lot of exciting reforms going on, and this is one of those that can really strengthen both local democracy and basic ownership rights. There is good reason to look at different models of land management and protection and to have a critical review of the role of the State in this administration.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-12-09.ana.ud433e3674	I am sure that all hearts are happy that I have been filled with speaking time. The Norwegian debate on immigration and integration is very often reduced to a change of words from the trenches, where the main theme is how many asylum seekers we should have. There will not always be an equally nuanced debate. Norway is not an isolated island in the world, we are part of a world that is getting smaller, or said in another way: a world where globalisation is strongly increasing, and where migration is also increasing. In the last week, the focus has been on the long-term asylum children and a lasting arrangement for them. That is an important part of the debate. We record that the government parties, the Christian People's Party and the Left have reached an agreement on a new regulation for the long-term children and their parents. It is good for the parties that they have finally come to an agreement, so that the people and the children involved can clarify their situation and move on with their lives. We are hoped that the cooperating parties will achieve what they want and avoid undesirable effects. The consequences of actual changes in the practice of non-resident management remain to be seen. It is positive that the number of quota refugees has increased political attention in the public vocabulary in the last year. I am pleased that the Christian People's Party and the Left have now pushed the government to increase the number of quota refugees up to the level proposed by the government of Stoltenberg II for 2014. We have the equivalent in our 2015 alternative budget for the number of refugees. There are, however, two aspects I want to deal with in relation to quota refugees. One is the request decision from Stortinget which was taken in June, where Stortinget asked for an assessment of how many more Syrian refugees we can receive in 2014 and 2015. In addition to an estimate of a number of challenges and a proposal to continue the level of the year for the number of refugees, the government is not included in the Grand Duchy’s assessment. The second thing I want to address about the quota refugees is related to the cooperation agreement in the foreign affairs field between the government parties, the Left and the Christian People's Party. Paragraph 24 states that priority should be given to quota refugees who are most likely to be successfully integrated. It represents a shift in Norwegian policy – a shift in the wrong direction. Despite the fact that the guidelines in force provide for such a priority, Norwegian practice has been that the request of the UN High Commissioner for Refugees has been followed. The fact that the Progress Party is in charge of such a policy is nothing new, but I think it is alarming and disappointing that the Right, Left and Christian People's Party actually accept this shift. The Workers' Party builds its refugee and asylum policy on international commitments and the moral duty we have to provide humanitarian assistance to our fellow human beings, including those outside the borders of the country. In the government, we have led a consistent, fair and legal refugee and asylum policy. That's what we're going to continue to do in opposition. The alternative to a consistent asylum policy is less predictability and undermining the institution of asylum. It has been an objective for us to ensure that as many as possible asylum seekers in Norway are actually entitled to asylum. You've succeeded in increasing that proportion. A percentage of more than 60 people have now been granted asylum. It has been widely agreed that rapid processing is important to control asylum immigration. I hope that the government will be able to continue this positive development and leave the foundation and succeed in creating under the previous government. For the Workers' Party, immigration and diversity represent an opportunity and a great potential for the nation. The assumption, of course, is that it is possible to facilitate a high degree of integration. That integration is to be based on the foundations of our society, and the welfare society in Norway is based on solidarity and willingness to provide and share. Trust in the institutions of society and trust among its citizens is a necessary prerequisite for this society. All obligations to end the laws of society and respect the democratic fundamental values. Now we've got the most right-wing government we've had in many years, and integration is not as high on the agenda anymore. This government is already starting with receptions, they are proposing reduced rates for adult residents in reception. This happens without any professional evidence and despite the fact that health professionals have previously warned against it, the rates are already too low for the residents to get enough nourishment. The Labour Party believes that there should be a sobering standard of stay in the reception of asylum, but not that one should be starved. So we're going to reverse this in our alternate budget. A strong welfare state contributes to good integration. Childcare facilities for all, a good public common school, safe health and care provision, social housing policy and a diverse cultural effort are therefore important for the success of the integration offer. The government's answer is to propose to cut the grant of the nursery provision for four- and five-year-olds in the host municipalities. There is no factual justification other than to balance the budget. We believe that children in reception centres, in the same way as Norwegian children, should have childcare facilities. It is a good start to integration and a reasonable offer also to those children who may not be allowed to be. In this context, I refer to our alternative arrangement for kindergartens in our budget. Nor is travel allowance for family reunification a priority of the government. The settlement of refugees we are all concerned with. However, the question is how the government will solve the challenge. The majority indicate in the timing that the integration grant and the investment grant increase. I'm not sure if that's enough. The forecasts show that next year's housing needs are roughly the same as in the current year, and this year it is estimated that 7800 out of 11,000 people have settled in need of housing. For the Workers' Party, we follow up our initiative from the revised national budget and propose a time-limited additional integration grant of NOK 100 000 per long-term refugee in reception. We must prevent the development of a class-based society in which people with a migrant background have a worse living conditions and lower social participation than the rest of the population. Work gives individual economic autonomy and is the main means of combating poverty, equalising social differences and achieving equality between women and men — including among immigrants. In particular, it is important that more women with a migrant background are given the opportunity to participate in the labour market. Although we have small differences and come out relatively well in terms of countries we compare with, it is still the case that immigrants are less represented in professional life than the majority population – 63.1 pst, respectively. for immigrants and 68,6 pst. for the majority population. Job opportunities, free core time in kindergarten and free part-time space in the school leisure system are examples of measures that contribute to integration. The question is what measures the government prioritises in order to raise participation in professional life. What are the priorities of the government to increase women's participation in the labour market? In the 1960s, Norway was very poor when it came to equality for women, but we made some political choices. In the 1970s–1980s and 2000s, Norway was raised to be one of the most equalised countries in the world, with a very high level of equality for women. This was the result of political elections. Policy choices must also be made in the field of immigration and integration. The political choices must be made so that immigrants have the opportunity to participate in social and professional life, in line with the rest of us who live here. Let me stress that successful integration is based on reciprocity in rights and obligations. It is crucial for good social development that citizens are able to take responsibility for their own lives, and the primary responsibility will always lie with the individual. At the same time, however, authorities must help to give new citizens a chance to become part of Norwegian society. Integration is a two-way process, based on duties and rights. It's about building barriers where they have to be found, whether it's in education, in work, in health, in the provision of services or in other areas. The barriers may be high or low, depending on the individual’s starting point. There is a difference between coming to Norway as a migrant worker, a family immigrant or a refugee. It's different for adults than for children. There are differences between men and women and between boys and girls. There are also differences between immigrants from different countries and continents. Therefore, measures must also be flexible, as individualised as possible and cover all phases of life. For example, there is a difference between Africans, who have a employment rate of 41.9 p.p., immigrants from the EU, who have an employment rate of 73 p.p. and those from Asia with an employment rate of 55,2 pst. And then there's some simple explanations of that. There are also some more complex ones. What remains to be seen, and which I hope the debate will clarify today, is that today's government will follow up with an active policy to continue to ensure a high level of participation and great opportunities for those who come to Norway. One important reason why we have done so well with integration is that we have had an overall policy of balancing differences. This government is pursuing a policy that helps to increase the diversity of society. It will also increase the challenges of successful integration. Increased disparities in society will weaken opportunities for immigrants. It will also mean poorer integration and thus waste possible resources for the nation. Worst of all, this will lead to a worse life and worse opportunities for those who come to Norway to get a better life and rot here. For me, there is little in the government budget that indicates that integration is a priority area.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-12-09.ana.ud433e4071	The centre party will pursue an immigration policy that puts human dignity in the focus. This has led to difficulties for municipalities that have contributed to the housing of lone minors in financing the measures considered necessary for many in this group. In addition to spending money to increase the reception of refugees, the Centre's proposal has provided funds to cover the cost of health care. We believe that the government needs to go through the guidelines on the reception of quota refugees and remove restrictions on the reception of refugees on health grounds.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-12-09.ana.ud433e4337	I will first be allowed to refer to the budgetary agreement between the government parties, the Christian People's Party and the Left and believe it is a very well-balanced budget in this area as well. It is an important political goal for the government to ensure that we have an effective and high-quality foreign administration, which ensures an effective, comprehensive and secure asylum chain. The draft budget and the agreement in Stortinget build well under this ambition. In the field of foreigners, three main objectives have been identified: fewer asylum seekers without protection, faster return of former asylum seekers and faster identification. Unfortunately, many people who live in Norway are illegal. It is important to have a close link in asylum policy, so that persons who do not have a need for protection are returned as soon as possible after rejection and those who are allowed to stay are integrated as quickly as possible. On 31 October 2013, just after the government change, there were 5,634 persons without a legal stay in the reception of asylum. On 31 October 2014, after a year of new government, there were 3,727 persons without a legal residence at the reception of asylum. So it's going to decrease by 1,907 people or 34 pst. In 2015, a significant increase of 82.5 million was provided to the police. kr to increase the number of shipments. In total, more than 10 000 people are scheduled to be returned in 2015. However, I would like to stress that it is not just money that contributes to the efficiency of the foreign administration. UDI, PU and UNE set in motion in the spring a great deal of cooperation to streamline the handling of so-called simple Dublin cases. Already now we see an incredibly good result of that cooperation. The case-handling time has decreased from about 175 days to about 175 days. 55 days. There is a reduced processing time of almost 70 pst. By contrast, it frees 500 places in reception facilities and reduces the number of people living in Norway for 4 months. 50 pst. fewer applicants disappear during the application procedure. I am very pleased with the efforts and cooperation with UDI, UNE and PU in this matter. It shows in practice that it is possible to dissolve in old inherited structures and find solutions jointly. The government has proposed 90 new closed reception points on Trandum in addition to the establishment of a new exit centre on Gardermoen. I am pleased that these measures are now widely supported in the Storting. One of the main challenges we face in the field of foreign affairs is the challenges of establishing the right identity. This is partly because some people deliberately and actively want not to clarify their own identity and mislead the foreign agencies, and partly because some asylum seekers come from countries where no satisfactory ID is issued, or because they have fled and have no ID papers. In particular, in the former cases, a substantial effort is made by the police to clarify the correct identity, which is a prerequisite for the proper handling of the application and for the return if residence is not granted. Therefore, the government has proposed strengthening the ID work and capacity of police investigation and foreign station work and competence in the field of ID. While it is important for the government to ensure that persons who do not have a legal residence in Norway are returned, it is also important to ensure that those who need protection receive it. The government proposes to continue the current quota refugees scheme where 1 000 seats were earmarked for Syrian refugees for a total quota of about 1 000. 1 700. The refugee disaster in Syria is of enormous dimensions, and very many people are in a terribly difficult situation. During the budget negotiations in Stortinget, the number of quota refugees has increased by a further 500 places. Compared to other European countries, Norway assumes a great responsibility for refugees in the region. At the same time, I would like to recall the importance of providing humanitarian aid in the neighbourhoods. Norway also contributes well. Then I would like to add that there has been a discussion, especially in part of the line changes, where one tries to present it as if the agreement between the Christian People's Party, the Left and the government parties must be either restraints or embellishments. I do not agree with that, because the agreement covers both. In some areas, restrictions have been introduced; in others, decorations have been provided. That is the point of this kind of agreement, so that all the parties in such an agreement will have an impact on the most important part of their policy.

It may not be so strange, because this debate has evolved more and more into a seminar – not only on what kind of label the parties of government and the Christian People's Party and the Left will put on the policies of the Workers' Party, but also on whether their own policies are a tightening, liberalisation or both. The truth is that the government, together with its cooperation parties, is essentially continuing the red-green immigration policy they like to criticise. However, there are some important exceptions here, including: that today’s government will give priority to the most easily integrated quota refugees at the expense of the most vulnerable refugees. I have also recorded that in relation to the issue of quota refugees, attempts have been made here in the Chamber to present it as if the Workers' Party is receiving more quota refugees. It's completely wrong. In our plan for next year, we have 500 more quota refugees. It is also worth noting that in the 2014 budget, the bulk of the population cut 500 quota refugees from the budget provided by the red-green government. In the spring, when the Workers' Party proposed a separate Syria billion with greater efforts in Syria's neighbourhoods, plus being able to receive more quota refugees in Norway, the bulk of the population also voted down. So, when SV in our promoted Document 8 proposal to accept several Syrian refugees, it ended up that the majority of the population made a request decision, asking the government to come back with an assessment of how many more quota refugees from Syria Norway could accept. The idea behind this was that we could then have a common starting point for these debates in the future – instead of adjusting the rate of quota refugees up and down slightly according to how the atmosphere is in the hall at all times, one could have a slightly more common approach for the future. It would give a completely different predictability. I had actually expected the government to follow up, and when the Minister of Justice here says that they have accepted that proposal by proposing next year in the draft budget zero more quota refugees from Syria, I will consider it a provocation. The Stortinget must come back to this at a later stage. So to the storytelling also in another area: our government settled in the order of 30,000 refugees in the last four years we had the government power. The housing policy is built on stone, and I really want with all my heart that councillor Horne will succeed in settling even more. We fully agree that all municipalities should be asked to accept refugees. I think it is completely unnecessary to make this a political conflict that does not exist. For many small municipalities, immigrants and refugees can be an opportunity to increase the population and give impetus to new growth. On the other hand, we are critical that the government has increased the proportion of lone minor asylum seekers. The creation of a meagre municipal economy for next year, we believe, will make it more difficult to settle refugees than it would otherwise have been. We call for a more offensive integration policy. Last but not least: the world now sees a precarious refugee situation. 51 million people are fleeing. We need more and better answers to the situation than we have today.

We are strengthening the work against coercion and sexual mutilation. We're doing the bets of the times on housing, so that people can quickly start their new life and live in their own homes. She can make friends of her own age, start school and start the journey to become an independent and strong woman. This budget ensures that the same girl’s mother can get a new start in life, where through language training she finds the way to work and can earn her first money ever. This budget also ensures that, by strengthening the work against coercion, we ensure that girls who are on their way at full speed to their own future do not get a rough and brutal stop on that journey. Finally, I have heard this saying many times: If you train a boy, you have trained a boy, but if you train a girl, you train an entire village. Perhaps it is that if a woman is integrated into Norwegian society, maybe the whole family has become too.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-12-09.ana.ud433e4760	Let me start by thanking the Committee – and, to this extent, the State Council, Anundsen – for a flexible approach to finding a solution. This is a difficult matter, there are difficult balances to be made, and there have been discussions among the parties in the committee. The Committee is essentially divided in several ways, so I'm going to be careful to be brief and just explain the main issues in the case and the Labour Party's position here. I expect the respective parties to explain both their views and their vote on the matter. This case concerns the possibility of granting exemptions from the obligation of professional secrecy to employees or other persons performing work or services in reception and care centres, so that information can be provided to the foreign authorities in order to better inform matters relating to the treatment of the foreign law. There are two issues which are being discussed in this matter and which are covered by the proposal: the adults and the minors. Already here, the committee separates layers, so I'm going to go over to explain the Workers' Party's position. For the Workers’ Party, this has been a difficult balance – weighing the need to provide information relevant to matters covered by the Foreign Affairs Act and the discussion of whether there is a basis for a stay in Norway, against the interests of privacy. When you are at a reception, the reception is to be regarded as one's home during the period you live there. Under the European Convention on Human Rights, there are quite strict restrictions to be able to disregard in Norway the interests of privacy — one’s home and privacy — but also the data protection law. We have come to the conclusion, after an overall assessment, that there are serious reasons to support the government's proposal to allow exemptions from the obligation of professional secrecy for adult residents. When it comes to the issue of lone minors living in reception, childcare or care centres, we have come to a different conclusion. The main reason is that in the case of minors, i.e. those who have not reached the age of 18, there is, in the view of the Workers' Party, a risk of mixing roles – the relationship between being a caregiver, since the child does not have any other caregivers when it comes here as a single minor, and being employed or performing services for the foreign administration. Therefore, we have come to the conclusion that this possibility of exemption from professional secrecy cannot apply to lone minors living in reception, care centres or child protection institutions. On this basis, together with the Center Party, we have made a proposal that I am addressing. I will not extend this procedure further. I expect that we will get a proper treatment, but I will again take the opportunity to thank you for a good cooperation in the matter.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-12-09.ana.ud433e4785	It is important that we guarantee the legitimacy of the asylum institution. All parties have an interest in determining asylum applications on the correct basis, not least the applicant himself. The government’s proposal can provide a greater room and opportunity to identify various types of asylum and residence errors. This will ultimately benefit the real asylum seekers. The decision is based on a thorough assessment of the facts in each case by the non-resident authorities to determine whether there is a basis for residence. The applicant has an obligation to do its best to contribute and provide the necessary documentation, while at the same time having to have systems that contribute as much as possible to getting the realities on the table. If we cannot capture those who are trying to exploit the institution of asylum or those who are actually persecuted, who have a real need for protection, and send them back to danger, none of the elements are a sustainable situation. That is why it is so important for us to clarify all aspects of an asylum application. Norwegian asylum recipients staff possess a knowledge that may be extremely relevant in an asylum process. In other words, information held by employees may be important in order to confirm or withdraw information provided by the applicant to the foreign authorities and, where appropriate, to provide a basis for further investigations. This may, for example, be the language the asylum seeker talks to daily, family or other information that can help to obtain the correct factual basis. It is important to stress that this could not only help to prevent those who provide incorrect information in order to cheat for stay, but also those who need real help and are actually persecuted. It is also important that we do not create an artificial distinction in government, rules that create different results for users, depending on the institution on which they are randomly placed. It is therefore important that we have equal rules for both public and private asylum recipients. Equal treatment in public administrations must be a matter of course, and I am pleased that the proposition provides for equal treatment of public and private asylum recipients. Then I had hoped that we could reach agreement today, and there is a great deal of agreement on the basis of that.
