ParlaMint-NO_2014-12-09.ana.ud433e4808	The government's proposal to provide staff in reception centres and child protection workers working with lone minor asylum seekers, the right and obligation to assist the foreign authorities with information relevant for the assessment of residence permits is a difficult one. The Committee has also failed to reach a conclusion which a majority can endorse. I think it is unfortunate that today we have different provisions on the professional secrecy of employees in private and public-run asylum recipients. Administrative law on professional secrecy, such as the Government through Prop. 104 L proposes changes to, applies to public activities and thus to municipally driven asylum recipients. Therefore, there is good reason to make the amendments necessary to harmonise the legislation. The body of the Centre joins the majority of the Committee on the issue of providing the foreign authorities with the necessary access to information which gives the authorities the best possible basis for determining each case. We believe that it is right and reasonable that employees of private and public-run asylum recipients should have the same right and obligation to provide information that they know about their identity and background, or about other matters of direct relevance to the assessment of their right to asylum. It is when we come to the question of professional secrecy for employees working with minors that we do not reach agreement with the government parties. Together with the Workers' Party, we oppose the right and obligation of employees responsible for lone minor asylum-seekers in asylum-seekers and care centres to provide information they may have acquired about the minor. We point out in our remarks that several of the hearing bodies are concerned about the provision of treatment to children if adults around them are required to provide information that they have been made aware of by virtue of their role of care. I note that the Council of State in its letter to the Committee agrees that the need for the safety and care of minors must be given great importance, but she believes that the need to provide important information to the foreign authorities should be given greater importance. That's the balance we in the Center Party don't agree with. The best of children has become a central and difficult concept of immigration policy. This is also such a case. Children are vulnerable, and I have noticed that in general, it is not expected that children will contribute to clarifying the basis for the application for a stay for themselves and their families. Lone minor asylum seekers are in an even more difficult situation than young people who have come here with their families. The provisions of the Children’s Act on the Confidentiality of Employees are stricter than those of the Administrative Act. I believe that we are on safe ground when we give lone minor asylum seekers the same legal protection as other minors.
ParlaMint-NO_2014-12-09.ana.ud433e4827	It is a human right to seek asylum, and we have a well-functioning rule of law and a responsibility for the proper handling of asylum applications and not least the proper treatment of asylum seekers. In the view of the Left, the legislative proposal promoted by the Government seriously weakens the right of asylum seekers to security. It is foreseen that employees in reception at child protection centres, etc. has an obligation to provide information about a resident and to do so on his own initiative. It is a form of a statutory declaration in which information has been collected by employees in their jobs at receptions is communicated to the foreign authorities – without informing and allowing residents to attend or correct. In our view, employees at reception are to ensure that life becomes as viable as possible to residents, who in many cases live there over several years, but are now scheduled to be designated by the foreign authorities. It doesn't seem like the Left is anything special. I would assume that living in a reception – with life waiting – in itself is quite stressful. There is a system in which, in what is one's own home indefinitely, one should go around and be afraid of what one says, and what one does, because it can be passed on to the foreign agencies, with a crucial importance for one's ability to stay in the country. In the view of the Left, it is not a legal State worthy. Who else in a society can be subject to a similar monitoring regime? Data protection fears that the law facilitates unqualified guessing, with then poor quality of information, which may have serious consequences for individuals. The Association of Advocates considers that the proposal violates all public administrative practices for the collection of personal data, where the record in the file limits the possibility of disclosure of data. The Ministry of Children and Gender Equality stresses that it is important that asylum seekers have confidence in reception staff and that it is particularly important for children and their sense of belonging and security. Some Norwegian human rights centres, such as NOAS, are critical, the Children's Ombudsman is critical, etc. The left shares the government's desire to facilitate the emergence of relevant information on the matter, but it strongly rejects the methods that it wishes to use. The goal does not at any time consecrate the instruments. And as pointed out by several hearing bodies, one cannot be sure that information disclosed is neither correct nor complete. The case will not be fully disclosed, but it will definitely be even more difficult to live in reception, both for adults and for young people. So an explanation of vote on what we have in front of us in relation to the various voting positions: we want to vote in principle against it all, but we will vote subsidiaryly for the Labour Party and for the Central Party's proposal for the second paragraph if it becomes a majority for the first paragraph, and then there must be separate voting over the first and second paragraphs.
ParlaMint-NO_2014-12-09.ana.ud433e4844	SV shares the view that the left now presented in the matter. We agree on remarks in the vote, and we also want to vote against both paragraphs, but subsidiary vote for the Labour Party and the proposal for the Second paragraph of the Center Party. I will go through some of the heavy objections that the hearing bodies have raised, because it is very rare that I have seen a case that has such serious objections. For example, several departments are opposed, for example: The Ministry of Renewal and Administration, which says – quite correctly – that it is not necessary to share all information on the matter with the foreign authorities, because that is not the case. The Data Protection Authority ‘fears that the law facilitates unqualified guessing, with then poor quality of information, which may have serious consequences for individuals’, and that is what is important in these cases. This is about very serious things. One thing is if you get to stay. The second is what happens if you are sent back again on a completely false basis. Here you give almost some kind of obligation to state people who have very different qualifications and different opportunities and knowledge to interpret the information they receive – it's not an interpreter, and it can be quite random what it is that you capture and interpret. Several hearing bodies also point out that there is information without interpreters and that interpretations will also be experienced. The association of asylum-seekers refers precisely to this and says: ‘When everyone has the right to report, the quality of the information will be influenced by randomness and by subjective perceptions. The association therefore considers that the draft law is contrary to the principle of non-discrimination, which should exist in foreign affairs.» I thought we agreed that the principle of non-discrimination should apply, and that it should not be accidental what information could be provided. Then there are even stronger objections from many of the hearing bodies regarding children. That is why I am pleased that an alternative proposal has been tabled today. Because it's clear that children who live in reception, in a much stronger way than adults, will depend on being able to trust some adults, trust them, tell thoughts, and maybe even stories that are not meant to be passed on to the alien authorities. These children also have no obligation, as has been said here. In the interests of the integrity and privacy of children, it should not be possible to provide information to the foreign authorities without consent. The Norwegian Centre for Human Rights believes that by introducing such an information obligation as is now proposed by the Government and for which it appears to be a majority, ‘employees in reception and care centres are deprived of the possibility of carrying out their own assessment of the child’. That's a reason we're obliged to take, and as the employees there are obliged to take. The Children’s Ombudsman considers that: ‘it is very worrying that employees in receipt shall be obliged to disclose information taken out of their context. Employees will come into an even more difficult intermediate position, resulting in a further blending of roles as carers and as part of the foreign authorities’. This need to be both a caregiver and an inspector and to indicate at once is not a safe situation for those who are to live on reception. Facts on such matters are important, but facts need to be established through procedures that ensure legal certainty and ensure the quality of the assessments of the information provided. What you're doing now is opening up a system where there are unqualified people who are going to interpret statements, perhaps given in a desperate and difficult situation, which can be crucial in a matter that can actually be about life and death. That's SV against.
ParlaMint-NO_2014-12-09.ana.ud433e4875	I'll first say a few words for the last post. I think it's based on some misunderstandings, which are answered in the proposition, among other things. is not such that all types of information should be used by the representative Andersen called ‘donors’. I think it is a terribly strong word in such a type of case where the aim is to obtain the information that is relevant to dealing with an asylum application. There are strong limitations and only relevant information to be obtained in such a situation. There shall be a notary with regard to the transmission and receipt of this information and it is definitely not about unqualified guessing in any way. Of course, all the information obtained through this system needs to be quality assured, and many of the misunderstandings would be solved if you had read the entire proposition. Then I want to thank the case rapporteur and the other debaters for a neat and good debate, and a review of what others have pointed to is a complicated challenge. I am pleased that there is now a majority in favour of employees on reception of asylum and the right to provide information to the foreign authorities under certain conditions. There are shared opinions about lonely minors, to which I come back. It is central to have clear and uniform rules for reception staff in all types of reception, and I have noticed that the majority in the Committee agrees with this. At the same time, it is important to have rules to facilitate the optimum decision-making by the foreign authorities in order to take proper decisions in foreign matters. This is the central purpose of this proposal. A good basis for decision in asylum matters is primarily important to ensure protection for those people who need it, but also to prevent persons from being granted a residence permit in Norway on a false or incorrect basis. The proposal will thus help to ensure the legitimacy of the asylum institution and prevent abuse. I am also very concerned that foreigners without a legal stay in Norway will be returned quickly to their country of origin. The proposal allows the Police Department to obtain information about foreigners that may be the property of the receptions, e.g.: information relating to identity and contact with family in the home Member State. Then I would also like to stress that the government and I believe it is important that the care of minors' needs for safety and care be given great importance. It's one of the things that makes this a very complicated case. But at the same time, I believe it is important that the foreign authorities have sufficient decision-making grounds to assess the child's best interests. For single minor asylum seekers, this will include, inter alia: be particularly important to know any contact with caregivers in the home Member State and the place of residence of caregivers. It is necessary for the foreign authorities to be able to make an assessment of the actual best interests of the child in all relevant cases. In the opinion of the government, it is not appropriate for employees in reception and care units to withhold such relevant information on the basis of professional secrecy. So I've noticed, as many have also said in posts in the room today, that there have been some references to hearing bodies that have been critical. These include: underlined the importance of this for confidence between employees and residents. Questions are asked about the competence of employees to pass on information, counter-dictation, etc. I believe that these are relevant counter-arguments, but I also believe that the need to have these matters clarified as well as possible must go ahead. In addition, our information to residents could help to reduce potential adverse effects in order to protect the confidence of employees and lone minor asylum seekers. The government’s proposals are limited to information which is particularly important for the access of non-resident authorities. I would also like to state that the purpose of the proposal is not to obtain information in all cases, but only where it is necessary to inform the case. Furthermore, relevant information can be disclosed, not otherwise. The right of objection will be fulfilled through the general procedure of the Foreign Service relating to the information used in the decisions. Under current legislation, all asylum seekers are obliged to provide the necessary documentation and to assist in obtaining the necessary information to inform their own asylum case. The government believes it is very important to have all asylum cases informed as well as possible, including the cases of the lone minors. I have to admit that we also settle for the Workers' Party and the Senter Party's proposals that will improve this in very many areas, where the disagreement is between minors. Then I will finally be allowed to thank the committee for a good day of debate, and wish Christmas, even if there is a little time left.
